SLOMW’s Jen Affleck Calls Out Zac Affleck’s Behavior in Unseen Videos

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Secret Lives of Mormon Wives’ Jen Affleck shared never-before-seen videos with Zac Affleck’s behavior on display during their marriage in the hours after he addressed their divorce.

In the wake of news that the Secret Lives of Mormon Wives star filed for divorce from her husband Zac Affleck after seven years of marriage—and after he shared a five-minute video responding to their split—Jen is sharing a look at past moments from their relationship.

In the first never-before-seen video, Jen wrote over the clip, “POV: your [sic] two weeks postpartum and trying to become an influencer.”

While standing in a kitchen in her bathrobe, an off-camera Zac tells Jen, “I hope you’re not making a TikTok looking like that. Your boobs are literally—your tits are sagging all over.”

As Jen laughs in response, he adds, “You don’t want to post that, baby, I’ll tell you that right now.”

She then asks her husband, “Am I embarrassing myself?” To which Zac responds, “A little bit.”

Jen—who shares three kids with Zac and is currently pregnant with their fourth baby—captioned the TikTok, “He was never my biggest supporter.”

In a second video, filmed in their bathroom, Zac asks Jen why she left a towel on the ground, and she notes it was dirty.

“Well, what am I supposed to shower with?” he asks his wife. “Is there no other towels?”

As he walks out of the bathroom, she responds, “I’ve been doing all your laundry for the past—forever. If I got a little bit more help, maybe it’d be nice. Maybe we’d have some towels.”

As she chuckles into the mirror, Zac glares at her from the doorway before moving out of frame.

The latest videos from Jen were posted shortly after Zac shared a video addressing his and Jen’s split. In it, he alleges that while she first brought up parting ways a year ago, and he’d encouraged her to give their marriage another shot, he learned of her divorce filing via TMZ.

"I don't want anyone speaking bad about Jen," he said in the video. "I'll be the first to say I've made plenty of mistakes in our marriage, and it just really was time for us to go our own ways. We got married so young, and it sucks because I really did feel like we had something pretty special when we got married."

He added, “When I first met Jen, she had this light about her, unlike anyone I've ever met. And she really does have such a good heart, and I will continue to support her and be her biggest fan.”

Within the comments of his TikTok, one user referenced Jen’s videos, calling Zac out for calling Jen’s boobs “saggy” postpartum.

“This was a video from 2022. Obviously horrible and should have never been said,” he replied. “When I saw the video today I was absolutely shocked I had said that. I said a lot of horrible and hurtful things but I have tried my best to learn and grow.”

When another fan referenced the same moment, he added, “I think that world already knows I made a lot of mistakes and said a lot of absolutely horrible things.”

Nevertheless, he hopes he and Jen can find their footing as coparents, especially as they prepare to welcome their fourth baby.

“I feel like probably the thing that's eating me alive the most is I feel like I failed my kids, and I'm worried that I'm going to cause them damage,” he said in his video. “I just hope that we can still give them a normal childhood, but as far as that goes, Jen and I are definitely on the same page of wanting to give them the best the best life we can."

Morning Minute: Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Morning Minute: Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

📉 Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

On Wednesday, the market decided a Democratic Congress was good news. Stocks had their best day in three months, Bitcoin climbed back over $80,800, and Trump posted on Truth Social that the market “just hit another Record High” with 401(k)s “through the roof.”

On Thursday, the market changed its mind.

The S&P 500 fell 4.36%, its worst session since the Liberation Day tariffs in April 2025. The Nasdaq dropped 5.52% and closed in correction, more than 10% below its record from last month. The Dow shed 1,833 points.

The damage concentrated in one place. The iShares Semiconductor ETF fell more than 12%, its worst day since March 2020. Micron lost 17.5%, AMD 17.3%, Marvell 16.4%, Nvidia 9.3%. The Magnificent Seven shed roughly $1 trillion in market value. The VIX touched 28.09 intraday, its highest since March. By the close, the U.S. stock market had lost $3T in value - more than the entire market cap of crypto.

Notably, the thing that spooked investors was the same election result that lifted everything a day earlier. Several states where Democrats won have active local opposition to data center construction, and the market spent Thursday pricing in slower permitting and tighter oversight of the AI buildout. Ipek Ozkardeskaya at Swissquote put it simply: “We suspect there is going to be some scrutiny on this aggressive AI buildout.” Rate expectations moved too, with December hike odds falling to 70.1% from 82.7%.

Crypto did not sit this one out. Bitcoin fell 5.4% to $75,871, below where it traded before the election. Ethereum dropped 7.6% to $2,239, a steeper fall than the Nasdaq. XRP lost 9.8%, and Hyperliquid fell 4.9%. The CMC20 index fell 6.29%. Almost $1.1B in crypto longs were liquidated on the day, before a slight relief rally overnight.

Two assets held up. Zcash fell 0.8% and Monero 2.2%, both less than the broad market, and both remain up on the week. Over the past year, Zcash is up 146% and Monero 74% while BTC is down 26% and ETH down 34%. The privacy trade is clearly still alive and looking as strong as ever.

Friday’s October jobs report is next. After a week where the market read the same political outcome two opposite ways inside two sessions, it is fair to wonder how much anyone actually knows right now. We will find out soon…

Best Sci-Fi Shows to Watch Before Apple TV’s ‘Neuromancer,’ Ranked

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

There's still nearly three months to go before Neuromancer premieres on Apple TV, but science fiction fans have plenty of other shows to hold them over in the meantime. Sci-fi has been flourishing on the small screen in the last decade or two, and chances are even the most die-hard fan has missed something great recently. The streaming era has been great for shows that need more special effects and bigger budgets, not to mention the welcome trend of adapting great books into series rather than movies. For those impatient to see Case (Callum Turner) and Molly (Briana Middleton) set out on their heist, read on for five sci-fi series you can watch now that are great fits for Neuromancer fans.

Neuromancer by William Gibson is a 1984 novel credited with popularizing the cyberpunk subgenre. In it, Gibson established many of the genre's most enduring tropes and concepts, even coining terms like "cyberspace" in his writing. The book is the first domino that led to later works like The Matrix and Altered Carbon, to name a few. However, this list looks beyond cyberpunk to shows with the same kinds of themes as Neuromancer. These are sci-fi series that don't shy away from the ugly truths, but don't dwell on them either.

While Foundation is in a completely different subgenre of sci-fi than Neuromancer, there are a lot of points of comparison between the two. Foundation is adapted from Isaac Asimov's novel series by the same name, which is just as important to space opera sci-fi as Neuromancer is to cyberpunk. It's one of Apple's biggest and most ambitious productions, and it may give us some important hints about where the streamer is taking Neuromancer. Showrunners David S. Goyer and Josh Friedman have not been shy about making additions or changes to the source material, and it has worked out well for them. We may see similar strategies here.

Foundation takes place in a distant future where humanity has colonized much of the galaxy and forged it into a massive empire. The empire is so vast that it's not even clear to everyone when it begins fracturing, but one prescient scientist develops a means of seeing ahead. He and his students seek to hold off the worst of the entropy and minimize the dark age between the fall of this empire and the rise of something new. The result is a story that spans planets, centuries, and even millennia.

The genre-bending 3 Body Problem is a great series to pick up in these final weeks of waiting for Neuromancer. The show is adapted from a trilogy of sci-fi novels by Liu Cixin called Remembrance of Earth's Past, with the first volume titled The Three-Body Problem. The books were originally published in Mandarin, with English translations by Ken Liu published from 2014 to 2016. The series is about earth's first contact with an alien civilization, and it's a brutal examination of how and why intergalactic connections might form.

The adaptation kicked off in 2024 on Netflix, and Season 2 was expected to premiere sometime in 2026. With no announcement so far the series may be delayed, but Season 2 has already been filmed and it has even been rumored that Season 3 was filmed alongside it, so more episodes are on the way. This is a perfect leapfrog situation for new viewers — you can dive into 3 Body Problem while waiting for Neuromancer, then by the time Neuromancer is over you may have new episodes of 3 Body Problem to enjoy.

Apple TV's Silo might have more in common with Neuromancer — at least in terms of genre and setting. The show takes place in a dystopian future where humanity seems to be confined to one underground shelter known as the silo. However, there are conspicuous mysteries about how they ended up there and what the world outside really looks like. The show masterfully teases out these mysteries, and I won't spoil anything good here. Suffice it say, this is a must-watch for fans of conspiracies, survival stories, and near-future sci-fi.

"My name is Kathy H. I'm 31 years old, and I've been a carer now for over 11 years."

Silo doesn't have the same cyberpunk flavor as Neuromancer and other stories in the genre, but it doesn't confine itself from those things either. The TV adaptation in particular does some fun things with technology and set design that will appeal to fans of cyberpunk. It also fleshes out its characters into the kinds of underdog heroes we all love to root for in these stories.

Cyberpunk is best known for its bold style, and no series brought as much cool-factor to the genre as Cowboy Bebop. The live action adaptation is available now on Netflix, while the original anime is currently streaming on Crunchyroll. Both are the perfect binge-watch before Neuromancer comes out. The series is a space western following bounty hunters on adventures throughout the solar system. The show has something for everyone — especially those who are already looking forward to Neuromancer. The anime is only 26 episodes long, so even if you've seen it before, a rewatch might be in order as we wait for something new.

Finally, of all the great sci-fi shows in the last two decades, nothing has evoked Neuromancer quite like HBO's Westworld. The show reimagined the dystopian theme park setting from the original 1973 film, filling the screen with action while tackling existential questions about the nature of consciousness and humanity. Westworld is a must-watch show today as it criticizes big tech in a way that seems more relevant with each passing year. Sadly, the series was removed from HBO Max a few years ago, and is only available today on Blu-ray. Still, it's worth seeking out.

There are plenty of other great sci-fi stories to check out as we wait for Apple TV's Neuromancer to bring the original novel to life. The show premieres on January 22 on Apple TV. In the meantime, Gibson's novels are currently available in print, digital, and audiobook formats.

Nico O'Reilly update: England omission explained and why it benefits all parties including Man City

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

By Oliver Thomas | 06 Nov 2026 13:27

Nico O'Reilly has been left out of the latest England squad due to fitness-related issues, Three Lions boss Thomas Tuchel and Manchester City manager Enzo Maresca have confirmed.

The 22-year-old was selected as England’s first-choice left-back for the 2026 World Cup and helped the Three Lions progress to the semi-finals in North America.

O’Reilly has earned 13 senior caps for England and has featured regularly under Tuchel, but a disrupted period at club level with Man City has led to his omission for November’s international break.

A midfielder by trade who has morphed into a left-back, O’Reilly has played the full 90 minutes just twice for Man City this season and has started seven games out of 15 in all competitions.

O’Reilly has predominantly been dealing with a persistent back problem that he sustained at the beginning of September, while a knee issue has also been causing him some discomfort.

Following England’s squad announcement on Friday morning, Tuchel discussed his full-back options ahead of UEFA Nations League fixtures against Croatia and Spain, and explained why O’Reilly will not be involved.

Inter Milan’s Djed Spence has returned to the senior fold at the expense of O’Reilly, while Lewis Hall and Jarrad Branthwaite are two other left-footed defensive options called up by Tuchel.

Meanwhile, Trent Alexander-Arnold, Jarell Quansah and Ben White are all set to battle for starts at right-back, with the latter returning to the Three Lions squad after an eight-month absence.

"Djed [Spence] is on the left side, we left Nico out because he's not fully, fully fit and needs to take his time to get fully fit and take care of his knee,” Tuchel told reporters.

"[Alexander-Arnold] was very good in the last camp, very good in the matches he played. Competition is on and he competes with Jarell and Ben for the position of right full-back."

Speaking at a press conference on Friday ahead of Man City’s Premier League trip to Nottingham Forest on Saturday, Maresca also shared his thoughts on O’Reilly’s fitness and his England omission.

"With Nico it's quite easy, some more problems, unfortunately. He's not 100%,” said the Citizens boss.

"In the last game (against RB Leipzig on Wednesday). I really liked him; I think he was playing a very good game, and this is what we need from Nico. Keep him fit because we know how good he is, and no doubt that in the future he will again be with the international team."

Asked whether O'Reilly needed rest over the international break, Maresca replied: "It’s what he needs, to use that break to do the right things in terms of therapy and this kind of things and be 100% over the international break”

O’Reilly’s absence from the England squad may well prove to be a blessing in disguise, as a much-needed break offers the youngster a crucial window to focus entirely on his recovery and return to full fitness.

Rather than risking a further setback on the international stage, the hiatus allows the starlet to build up his sharpness in a controlled environment, ensuring he is fully prepared for the gruelling club football schedule awaiting Man City.

With Maresca’s side facing a notoriously busy winter fixture list – 11 games before the end of the year – having a rejuvenated O'Reilly fresh and ready for domestic duties will provide a massive boost to the Citizens' squad depth.

Furthermore, his omission serves a dual purpose internationally, granting Tuchel the perfect opportunity to assess alternative left-back options and experiment with England's defensive depth in the closing stages of their Nations League group-stage campaign.

Hall is the most likely candidate to start at least one of England’s matches against Croatia and Spain this month, while Spence and Branthwaite provide alternative left-sided cover.

It remains to be seen whether O'Reilly will be involved against Nottingham Forest this weekend, or if he will instead return to action on November 21 when Man City take on Fulham in their first Premier League fixture after the international break.

The mystery of long COVID: What lasting health problems does the virus cause?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A massive study shows how comparison groups shape findings about long COVID and which lasting health problems are linked to the virus.

Long COVID can leave people feeling unwell long after an infection. But figuring out which health problems the virus caused depends partly on who researchers compare those patients with.

A Stanford University team found just how much that choice matters.

When the researchers repeated an approach used in earlier studies, it linked COVID to unlikely problems, including injuries from lightning, drowning, and electrocution.

Changing the comparison group produced a much shorter list of lasting effects. By the second year, none met the study’s cutoff, though that does not mean every patient had recovered.

The team drew on 14.4 billion insurance claims from nearly 245 million US patients.

These are bills from doctors, hospitals, and pharmacies, so they record medical care rather than how someone feels each day.

Between about four months and a year after infection, the revised approach linked 98% fewer health problems to COVID than the older method did using the same records.

Jure Leskovec, a Stanford computer science professor, was one of the study’s senior authors.

“This doesn’t mean long COVID is 98 percent less common. It means that how we design these studies fundamentally changes which health effects we can reliably attribute to COVID,” Leskovec said.

Many earlier studies compared people who tested positive with people who had no recorded COVID diagnosis. That second group included people who tested negative and people who never took a test.

Those groups may differ before COVID enters the picture. Someone might get tested during a visit for diabetes or another illness, or because they see doctors more often.

“If you compare people who tested positive for COVID with people who were never tested, you risk attributing differences in their underlying health and healthcare-seeking behavior to the virus itself,” Leskovec said.

The concern is that researchers could end up blaming COVID for problems linked to someone’s existing health or reasons for seeking care.

The team compared people who tested negative and never had COVID recorded with untested people who had similar medical histories.

Between one and four months later, 82.5% of the health problems tracked were at least 10% more common in the group that tested negative.

A negative test could not explain those extra problems; the researchers pointed to other factors associated with getting tested.

For the main analysis, follow-up began with each patient’s first PCR test between March 1, 2020, and March 1, 2021.

The team compared positive results only with negative results, so everyone had a reason to get tested.

The researchers then adjusted the comparison group to match the positive group’s medical history over the previous four years.

They also matched age, sex, and neighborhood measures such as income.

This matching approach is called a synthetic control. Co-senior author Stefan Wager, an associate professor at Stanford Graduate School of Business, said economists commonly use it.

The records came from Komodo Health, which gathers claims from insurers, pharmacies, and programs including Medicare and Medicaid.

The researchers followed patients for up to two years and examined 614 health outcomes.

“Even with 244 million patients, you can get highly precise answers to the wrong question. Study design matters as much as data size,” Leskovec said.

As an initial check, the revised method picked up familiar COVID symptoms during the first month of infection.

Hamed Nilforoshan, a Stanford computer science doctoral student and one of three first authors, also recreated the older approach, closely following a 2021 Nature study. “Something wasn’t adding up,” Nilforoshan said.

The team checked 49 outcomes that COVID could not plausibly cause, including firearm injuries and birth defects recorded in adults. The older approach linked COVID to more than half.

It suggested a 76% increase in a category of injuries that included lightning, drowning, and electrocution. It also suggested an 18.6% increase in adults receiving diagnoses for birth defects of the eye, ear, face, or neck.

The authors said chance or flaws in study design presumably explained those unlikely links.

The revised approach flagged just two of the 49 outcomes, both showing lower risk after a positive test, which suggests it may slightly underestimate some real effects.

For the main results, a health problem had to increase by at least 10% and pass a strict statistical check that accounted for hundreds of comparisons.

From day 30 to day 119, the revised method linked 43 health problems to COVID, compared with 262 under the older method.

From about four months to a year, the revised method found five, compared with 286 under the older method. In the second year, those counts were zero and 369.

On average, recorded health problems in the positive group fell to roughly the comparison group’s level within a year. Earlier US veterans studies had found harmful effects after two or three years.

During the first months, COVID remained linked to problems involving the lungs, heart and blood vessels, kidneys, immune system, muscles, gut, and nervous system. Hair loss also appeared.

The biggest relative increase involved myopathy, a muscle disease, with related insurance claims about three times as common after a positive test.

Hair loss claims were nearly twice as common, while palpitations, malnutrition, and fatigue were 12% to 21% more common.

Between about four months and a year, five problems still met the cutoff: myopathy, hair loss, two categories of blood clots in the lungs, and respiratory failure.

Pneumonia, shortness of breath, chest pain, and fatigue increased by less than 10%. Even small increases in such common problems can affect many people.

Steven Goodman, a professor in Stanford Medicine’s Department of Epidemiology and Population Health, compared the findings with illnesses that linger after other infections.

“These findings indicate that long COVID may be more similar to other extended post-viral syndromes than many have thought,” Goodman said.

Still, billing records can miss symptoms such as fatigue and brain fog.

The study also covered people who sought PCR testing through healthcare, leaving out infections identified only through home tests.

About 20% of the comparison group caught COVID within two years, which could make the virus’s effects look smaller. The study could not establish what caused symptoms that lasted beyond a year.

The authors stressed that lasting symptoms are real. Other illnesses that prompted the original test, or other factors, could help explain them, but these records cannot settle that question.

“These results should complement physicians’ clinical assessments, not replace them,” Leskovec said.

The team called for research into the biology of long COVID and biomarkers, measurable signs in the body that could help identify it.

They also suggested repeating earlier studies with comparison groups based on testing to see how much their results change.

The study is published in the journal Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences.

Northern lights could reach New York and Idaho this weekend as speedy solar wind races toward Earth

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A colossal coronal hole on the sun is unleashing a stream of high-speed solar wind that could spark impressive auroras Nov. 6-8.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The northern lights could put on an impressive display this weekend as a colossal coronal hole on the sun fires a stream of high-speed solar wind toward Earth. Minor (G1) geomagnetic storm conditions between Nov. 6 and Nov. 8, with a chance of moderate (G2) conditions on Nov. 7 and Nov. 8, according to NOAA's Space Weather Prediction Center (SWPC).

If G2 conditions materialize, the northern lights could be visible as far south as New York and Idaho.

The culprit behind the potential uptick in northern lights is a large coronal hole on the sun, a region where the sun's magnetic field opens up. They allow streams of charged particles known as the solar wind to escape more easily into space. Coronal holes appear darker in ultraviolet images because they are cooler and less dense than their surroundings.

And this particularly large — and rather oddly shaped — coronal hole is sending some seriously speedy wind our way.

The U.K. Met Office forecasts solar wind speeds between 342 and 404 miles (550-650 kilometers) per second, considerably faster than the typical speed of around 249 miles (400 km) per second.

The fast solar winds are expected to arrive around Nov. 6-7 and could persist through much of the weekend. It could interact with Earth's magnetic field, potentially triggering geomagnetic storms and fueling spectacular aurora shows. The stronger the storm, the farther south the northern lights can potentially be seen.

Under G1 conditions, auroras may be visible from northern Michigan and Maine, but if activity intensifies to G2 levels, skywatchers farther south, including those in New York and Idaho, could be in for a treat.

But remember! Aurora forecasts are notoriously tricky. Much depends on the strength and orientation of the magnetic field carried by the incoming solar wind, particularly whether it points southward, allowing more energy to transfer to Earth's magnetic field.

To give yourself the best chance of seeing the northern lights, keep an eye on those aurora forecasts, head somewhere dark away from city lights if you can, with a view of the northern horizon. To stay updated on space weather and know the best times and places to catch auroras, consider using a space weather app tailored to your location. One app I recommend is "My Aurora Forecast & Alerts," available on both iOS and Android. Another great tool is the "Space Weather Live" app on iOS and Android, which offers more in-depth insights into current space weather conditions and their potential for aurora activity.

Daisy Dobrijevic joined Space.com in February 2022, having previously worked as a staff writer for All About Space magazine. She completed an editorial internship with BBC Sky at Night Magazine and worked at the National Space Centre, communicating space science to the public.

Daisy holds a PhD in plant physiology and a Master's in Environmental Science. Based in Nottingham, U.K., she covers all things space, with a special focus on solar activity and space weather. She also has a keen interest in astrotourism and is always on the lookout for the next northern lights adventure.

She will join Hurtigruten as an onboard astronomer for a northern lights sailing in January 2027.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Sam C is a sports journalist with a growing reputation for producing insightful and engaging multimedia football content. With a background in presenting and digital storytelling, he has contributed to a range of high-profile platforms, including The United Stand and Sky Sports, where his work has reached millions across YouTube and social media.

His experience spans live presenting, video production, and article-based reporting, all of which are underpinned by a commitment to quality journalism and digital impact.

You can also find his work on platforms such as The United Stand: https://www.youtube.com/@UnitedStand

England manager Thomas Tuchel has revealed that Everton midfielder Kiernan Dewsbury-Hall came extremely close to earning his first senior international call-up, praising his performances and explaining where he sees the 28-year-old fitting into his system.

Dewsbury-Hall has enjoyed an impressive spell at Everton, establishing himself as an important player and continuing to push for international recognition.

Despite his performances, the former Chelsea and Leicester City midfielder was left out of Tuchel’s latest England squad for the upcoming internationals against Croatia and Spain.

His Everton teammate James Garner, however, was included once again, raising questions about how far Dewsbury-Hall remains from receiving his first senior opportunity.

Speaking at Wembley on Friday, Tuchel was asked by GIVEMESPORT reporter Sam Cohen about Dewsbury-Hall’s omission, particularly given his performances for Everton and previous involvement in England’s provisional squads.

The German was full of praise for the midfielder and admitted that he had been seriously considered for selection.

“Very good player, very good season, you can just see it,” Tuchel said.

“He’s a very good football player overall because I see him in the way we play now.

“I don’t see him in central midfield in the build-up and in the build-up of the game. I see him more in the finishing part of the game as an offensive eight or almost as a 10.”

Tuchel’s assessment offers an interesting insight into how England’s coaching staff view Dewsbury-Hall’s qualities.

Rather than considering him primarily as a deeper midfielder responsible for progressing possession, the England boss believes his strengths are better suited to operating further forward, where he can influence attacks in the final third.

However, that is also an area of the pitch where England have considerable competition for places.

“The competition is very, very high in our squad in this position,” Tuchel continued.

“But he is, as you know, he’s in our 55, a standout player for Everton and we see his quality.

He’s a nice watch because he’s a very, very good football player and was very, very close for me.

Those comments will offer encouragement to Dewsbury-Hall, who has previously spoken about being included in England’s provisional squads without making the final selection.

While Garner’s inclusion demonstrates that Tuchel is keeping a close eye on Everton, Dewsbury-Hall faces a different challenge because of the attacking midfield role the England manager believes suits him best.

Crucially, Tuchel’s comments suggest the midfielder is not being overlooked entirely.

Instead, he remains firmly within England’s wider selection picture, with his performances at club level continuing to attract attention.

For Dewsbury-Hall, the task now is to maintain his form and convince Tuchel that he deserves an opportunity in one of the most competitive areas of the England squad.

At $18,900, Is This Incredibly Low-Mileage 1986 Chrysler LeBaron Town & Country A Woody You Would Buy?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

With just 3,800 miles on the clock, today's Nice Price or No Dice LeBaron Town & Country is a true time capsule of '80s glory. It even has a bench seat in the front! Now priced about 30% more than when it was new, we'll have to decide whether it's worth it to feel the MTV-era vibes.

With the introduction of the Prius in the late 1990s, Toyota rewrote the meaning of a "hybrid auto." Before the Prius mated electric and internal combustion motors for power, a hybrid auto typically meant an American engine in a European car. The Sunbeam Tiger with its British-built body and Ford 289 was a hybrid. So too were the Iso Rivolta and the clutch of Bizzarrini, which sported Chevy small-block power.

The 2013 Cadillac Escalade we looked at on Thursday is a hybrid in the modern sense, featuring a gearbox employing two electric motors to aid the V8 engine in both driving the SUV and keeping its high-voltage battery full of electrons. That was, and still is, a fiddly system with a number of known failure points. On top of that, our Escalade already had 140K under its belt, so those failures could be expected at any given time. Those factors led to warnings in the comments and eventually, a 65% 'No Dice' loss for the Caddy's $11,995 asking price.

In the 1933 novel "Lost Horizon" by James Hilton, a small group of Westerners survives a plane crash high in a remote, uncharted part of the Himalayas. There, they discover a lamasery—Shangri-La—which seemingly has magical properties that slow aging among its inhabitants. This comes at a high cost: the inhabitants are trapped there, because leaving means rapidly aging and dying. I know, bummer.

Today's 1986 Chrysler LeBaron Town & Country looks like it was just magically freed from some mystical Shangri-La, as it appears in as-new, almost entirely original condition and has less than 4,000 miles on the clock. How did this come to be? The seller has a succinct recap in the ad:

Back in 1986 a man bought his wife the finest station wagon Chrysler produced, this LeBaron Town & Country TURBO wagon. She only drove it 1500 miles before parking it in the garage when she became ill, and ultimately passed. The car ended up in climate controlled storage at a Chrysler dealer for 30 years. Then during Covid it was brought back to life with a new gas tank, driven a couple hundred miles and ended up on consignment at World Wide Autos in Colorado.

The current owner bought the car four years ago, brought it to Georgia, and replaced all the rubber bits, fluids, and the non-working factory stereo. Other than those updates, this K Car is claimed to be just as it left the factory.

This Town & Country has the goods, too. An unexpected decal on the hood announces the engine: a 2.2-liter Chrysler four with fuel injection and a Garrett T03 turbocharger. The motor was factory-rated at 146 horsepower and 168 pound-feet of torque, which might not seem like all that and a bag of chips today, but back in the mid-'80s it was pretty impressive.

Befitting this car's role as a Town & Country cruiser, the motor is mated to a three-speed TorqueFlite automatic which drives the front wheels.

According to the ad, the car is 100% roadworthy and even has working R12 A/C. Before the current owner took possession, a previous owner replaced the gas tank. That, the rubber, and the radio are apparently the only hard bits of the car that aren't factory original. That makes this LeBaron a total time capsule, and the seller boasts it's a hit at many local car shows.

Of course, this also has the look that anyone with an "I HEART THE '80s" tattoo will simply gush over. There's an old joke about a traveler stopping into a bar in the South and asking what kind of music they play there, to which the bartender replies, "Oh, we got both types: country AND western." This LeBaron is similarly a Town & Country model, meaning it has the faux woodgrain paneling down its flanks emulating the classic Town & Country Chryslers of the 1940s. That's in amazing condition with no apparent fading or damage. Under that is a coat of elegant black paint that looks to be simple, single-stage. Chrome wire wheel covers complete the picture.

There's more old-school goodness in the cabin, as it features a wide split-bench seat in the front, clean gray cloth upholstery, and a low center tunnel afforded by the FWD layout that offers a ton of foot room.

This is a high-end K-car, so it has power windows and locks, cruise control, a tilt wheel, and the aforementioned A/C. The modern Sony stereo is a bit of a letdown, but is probably more useful than the non-working original, even if it spoils the overall throwback experience.

This LeBaron comes with a clean title, and the seller suggests that while museums are welcome to snap it up, the wagon should really be enjoyed on the road. That, of course, means adding to its miles, which takes away one of the car's unique ownership aspects. Maybe put it on a flatbed and drive it around so you can enjoy the experience without ruining that uber-low mileage fame.

Whatever the choice, we'll now have to consider LeBaron's $18,900 asking price. That's a good bit higher than the around $12K it cost when new, but inflation has taken its toll, and honestly, where are you going to find another one in this condition and spec? Supply and demand is one of the first things they teach you in Econ 101.

What do you think about this LeBaron and that $18,900 asking? Do you feel like a buyer should be resigned to pay that price given the car's rarity and condition? Or is that too much even if this LeBaron offers both Town AND Country?

Wolverine Loader II Review: No Break-In, No Blisters

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 6, 2026 1:24 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

New work boots can be miserable until they’re broken in, especially if you spend long days on your feet. My husband, a contractor who also works on our farm, tested the Wolverine Loader II HyperRest CarbonMAX boots for a full week, wearing them up to 10 hours a day. We had previously tested two of Wolverine’s Infinity System work boots, so we were curious to compare them with this pair’s comfort design. Here’s what we found. (And for more options, see our team-tested best work boots guide.)

The break-in was the biggest surprise of our test. My husband put the Wolverine Loader II boots on and went straight to work. There was no period of stiff leather rubbing against his ankles, no uncomfortable pressure around his toes, and no need to alternate with an older pair while the new boots softened up.

He wore them for a week while doing his regular contracting work and handling chores on our farm, including clearing trees and operating equipment. Some days stretched to 10 hours, but he never complained about sore feet afterward.

Wolverine credits much of that comfort to its (removable) HyperRest footbed, which cushions and supports the foot, and a polyurethane midsole that helps absorb the impact of walking on hard surfaces. The padded collar also helps prevent rubbing around the ankle. Whatever the combination, it worked well for him from the first day.

In our testing, yes. My husband is accustomed to wearing work boots, so he knows the difference between a pair that feels good in the store and one that remains comfortable through a full shift.

The Loader II passed that test. He especially liked how light they felt for boots with protective toes. Wolverine lists the weight at 2.04 pounds per boot, and the CarbonMAX composite toe provides impact and compression protection without the bulk of a traditional steel toe.

The rubber wedge outsole is another feature of the comfort design. Its broad contact area helps distribute weight on hard, flat surfaces, which can be particularly helpful for contractors who spend hours on concrete or other unforgiving flooring.

The Wolverine Loader II is more than just a comfortable boot. Its CarbonMAX toe meets ASTM F2413-24 impact and compression requirements, and the boot also carries electrical hazard and slip-resistance ratings.

The waterproof full-grain leather upper includes reinforced toe and heel bumpers, which help protect areas that often take a beating around tools, equipment, and construction materials. A breathable waterproof membrane and moisture-wicking lining provide additional protection in wet conditions.

Wolverine also uses Goodyear welt construction, which allows the boots to be resoled rather than discarded when the outsole wears out. That’s a welcome feature in a work boot intended for regular use.

One consideration is the wedge outsole. While it’s designed for dependable traction, its relatively flat profile didn’t dig into deep mud as aggressively as some heavily lugged soles. For workers who spend most of their time in muddy fields or on steep, loose terrain, outsole design deserves extra attention.

At $169.95, the Loader II is cheaper than the premium Wolverine Infinity System boots we previously tested, yet it delivered the comfort my husband needs for long workdays.

I think these boots are particularly well suited to contractors, carpenters, equipment operators, and other tradespeople who want safety-toe protection without stiff, heavy footwear. The 6-inch lace-up design also provides a more adjustable fit than a pull-on boot.

After a week of demanding use, my husband had no complaints about comfort, fit, or foot fatigue. That’s an excellent start, although we’ll need more time to judge how the leather, stitching, and outsole hold up over months of daily wear.

For workers who dread breaking in a new pair of boots, the Loader II might just be the ticket.

Ethereum devs won’t fork Glamsterdam on Hoodi until smart contract risks resolved

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Ethereum developers are investigating concerns tied to eight smart contract invariants tied to Glamsterdam’s EIP-8037 and EIP-8038.

Ethereum core developers delayed the Glamsterdam upgrade’s implementation on the Hoodi testnet until security concerns tied to eight smart contract invariants are resolved.

Some existing Ethereum projects rely on assumptions that certain operations would cost a specific amount of gas, which would be invalidated by Glamsterdam’s EIP-8037 and EIP-8038 because they change how gas is charged for certain operations, the Ethereum Foundation said in a Friday blog post.

The decision followed an Oct. 13 report from smart contract auditor ChainSecurity, which found that the two Ethereum Improvement Proposals (EIPs) could break smart contracts that rely on fixed gas-related assumptions when external calls fail.

Delaying the Hoodi testnet fork gives developers more time to identify potential smart contract issues and determine whether changes are needed before Glamsterdam progresses toward mainnet deployment. The Hoodi fork will not proceed until the issues are resolved, added the Ethereum Foundation’s post.

Ethereum’s Glamsterdam upgrade went live on the Sepolia testnet on Oct. 6, in one of its final major rehearsals. The upgrade aims to advance Ethereum’s layer-1 scaling roadmap with enshrined proposer-builder separation, block-level access lists and changes to gas pricing that better reflect the cost of execution, the Ethereum Foundation announced in a Sept. 28 blog post.

The Best Gifts for Hunters: Get Them What They Actually Want

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 6, 2026 1:22 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Get the hottest outdoor news from Outdoor Life.

Hunters can present a challenge when it comes to gift buying. They already have one of these or two of those. They don’t particularly care for Model A or Brand B. There’s always the $100 Gift Card, but you really don’t want to be that impersonal, now do you?

Fortunately, there’s an almost endless array of “I’d love one of those” gift ideas whether the hunter on your list chases waterfowl, big game, upland birds, or small game. This year, the following gift ideas will help you put the right hunting gear beneath the tree.

Missing a target means lost or broken arrows. Not only does that get expensive, it’s downright discouraging. The BlackOut LongShot 36 is a big target that allows bowhunters to confidently practice shooting their bows at extended ranges without worrying about missing the target. Best of all, it’s affordable. In my testing, I’ve found arrow removal is easy, even though I’m shooting small-diameter arrows over 290 fps.

Even if you’re shopping for a bowhunter who already owns a backyard target, they’ll still want and need this one. It’s a can’t-miss gift for anyone who loves flinging arrows.

Thermal monoculars are handy devices to have around the house and in the field. They can help you locate coyotes in the pitch dark, blood trail a deer (where legal) and make home repairs, such as finding heat leaking out of a window. The key to choosing a thermal as a gift is understanding that the more you spend, the better the image gets. But not all use cases require top-end performance. If someone is going to use their thermal to scan fields, recover game, and use around the property, a lower-resolution sensor works well. If they need to view and identify animals at longer ranges (beyond 100 yards), they’ll need a top-of-the-line unit.

The ATN BlazeSeeker 6 is a compact thermal that slips easily into a pocket and can record photos and videos. It costs only $429 to $489 and is ideal for everyday use around the property and in the field. If you’re buying for someone who needs more capability, I recommend the BlazeTrek 6. The BlazeTrek 6 has a better image and costs $795 to $1,499. Both monoculars are easy to use, and the ATN app has a handy AI assistant to guide you through any snags the recipient might encounter. Any hunter will be thrilled to receive an ATN monocular, and it’s another fantastic gift to consider.

Good gifts are practical, but the best gifts help people have fun. One of the keys to having fun in the outdoors is being comfortable no matter the conditions. The Uncompahgre Foundry Jacket is a highly versatile, packable layer that’s ideal for staying outside when the weather turns cold. From fishing to late-season hunting, this is an outer shell that will get a ton of use. We’ve tested and recommended the Foundry Jacket. You can read our full review to learn more.

Soft cases are the practical way to carry a shotgun to the range or to your hunting spot. They’re something that every bird hunter needs and will use regularly. They’re also relatively affordable, which makes them a great gift. The Evolution Outdoor Rawhide Classic Camo Shotgun Case is a stylish and practical case that’s made from waxed canvas. It will carry shotguns up to 50 inches in overall length, so it will accommodate most semi-automatics with 28-inch or shorter barrels and double guns with up to 30-inch barrels.

It’s tough buying the right knife for an outdoorsman, but you can’t go wrong with the iconic Victorinox Huntsman made by the folks who produced the original Swiss Army Knife™. The Huntsman has 15 functions, including blades, corkscrew, reamer, punch and sewing awl, scissors, wood saw, wire stripper, screwdrivers, and more. It’s available in 7 different color patterns. You can be sure that the outdoorsman on your list will find unlimited uses for this versatile Swiss-made tool through the many seasons that it will serve them.

Built to handle rough roads, bad weather, and air travel, this is a gift that tells someone you understand their passion and want to protect the tools that make it possible. It’s designed to hold two scoped rifles, with thick protective foam inside and six sturdy latches that keep everything sealed tight. Whether you’re shopping for a hunter that drives to the deer camp or flies out west for elk season, this case gives them peace of mind that their firearms will arrive zeroed and ready to hunt.— Scott Einsmann

You can get a turkey hunter a new call, a knife, or this piece of turkey hunting culture. The Tenth Legion is a book that captures the heart of what it is to be a diehard turkey hunter and is guaranteed to be enjoyed by anyone that chases gobbles throughout the spring. It was written by the legendary Colonel Tom Kelly, who recently passed on. I keep a copy of the Tenth Legion in my vest and read through it when the birds aren’t feeling talkative, and I highly recommend it as a gift for the turkey obsessed. — Scott Einsmann

Securely packaging your hard-won wild game, be it fresh venison, wild turkey, or mallard duck breasts has never been as easy as it is now, thanks to Cabela’s Deluxe 12 (D12) vacuum sealer. Weighing just four pounds, the D12 features a heavy-duty commercial vacuum pump designed for years of service. User-friendly, easy-to-clean, and programmable, the D12 takes up little counter space, while offering multiple modes of operation including manual seal, pulse, Auto/Vac, Standard, and Extended, as well as ‘Canister’ option great for preserving tough-to-seal foodstuffs.

This chest pack is designed to hold a hunter’s binoculars, and much much more. For backcountry hunters, a chest pack is a critical piece of gear and there are few out there that offer more features than the Kuiu Pro. It includes: magnetic closure for maximum quietness, adjustable main pouch for proper bino fit, rail-lock system for bottom accessory, rail-lock mount for ammo holder, plus a variety of extra pockets. It comes in five different camo/solid color options (I don’t think you can go wrong with solid Ash). This is an ideal gift for a big game hunter or even a turkey hunter, who needs quick access to optics and other critical gear.

A cellular trail camera is a perfect gift for any deer or turkey hunter. If the hunter already has a cell cam, they’ll be happy to have another (you can never have too many cameras out there). And if they don’t have camera yet, then your gift will get them in the game. And there’s not a trail camera out there that’s more effective and easy to use than the Tactacam 4.0. The Outdoor Life crew thoroughly tests trail cams every year, and the Tactacam 4.0 won the editor’s choice award, hands down, in 2026. Features include: Up to 4K photo definition, up to 96 feet of flash range, Low-glow or no-glow flash settings, 16 gb of internal storage, powered by 12 AA batteries, live video, on-demand photos, GPS enabled.

Sure, the description says ‘Waterfowl Backpack,’ and all duck hunters should have one of these, but don’t let the name fool you. This well-built large capacity pack has 101 uses in the field, e.g. camera bag, lunch box, blind bag, and dry clothes storage to name just a handful. With over 1,550 cubic inches of interior space, the Northern Flight backpack is crafted of rugged 600D polyester with a water-resistant polyurethane coating to help keep what’s inside nice and dry. There’s plenty of pockets, glove-friendly zippers, a huge main compartment, hydration pouch, and MOLLE attachment points for additional capacity. In other words this is an excellent hunting pack for a variety of scenarios.

How many times have you wished for some warmth while waiting out that trophy whitetail from inside the chilly confines of that late January ground blind? Thanks to the innovative minds at TideWe and their heated hunting chair, turning up the thermostat just became possible. At first glance, the chair seems ordinary enough – adjustable height; swiveling through 360 degrees; armrests; and a detachable gear pouch. But this chair adds a little spice in the form of battery-powered heat. Three heat levels (100 through 130 degrees), long 10-hour  battery life at the lowest setting, and a fast three-hour charge time make this ‘hot seat’ a Must-Have this winter.

Hunters constantly need to keep knives and broadheads sharp. This folding field sharpener is a versatile and handy way to maintain everything from pocketknives to axes. It’s only $13, and I’ve been testing it while sharpening broadheads and honing knives. It works great, and I recommend it.

*I noticed this product has only a three-star rating from customer reviews. But the reviews are all for a fishing reel. This appears to be a glitch on the Bass Pro Shops/Cabela’s website.

There’s nothing worse than slipping your feet into cold wet boots. Fortunately, the folks at PEET Dryer have the answer in their original boot dryer. The PEET dryer uses convection to push warm air upwards through specially designed chambers and ports to dry shoes and boots of all shapes, sizes, and material types. Safe enough to operate 24/7, this makes the perfect gift for anyone who goes outside, but especially hunters who are prone to get their boots wet.

Hunters often like to forage while they’re in the field. But wild mushrooms are nothing to be guessed at when it comes to their edibility. Some make wonderful meals, while others can make a person terribly sick, or worse. This makes a good mushroom identification book an absolute necessity for the forestland forager. And few are as comprehensive as David Arora’s All That The Rain Promises, and More. An educator in the field of mycology, Arora’s easy to understand descriptive prose, along with his excellent photographs, takes much of the mystery out of separating the ‘good’ from the ‘not so good’ when it comes to fungi.

Virtually indestructible. That’s the best way to describe YETI’s latest introduction, the Loadout Gobox 1 Gear Case. Built out of what the company refers to as ‘Defender II Material,’ the Gobox 1 is the perfect hard case if you’re looking to safely store items such as a wallet, passport, keys, portable power, or anything else. The Gobox 1 seals out Mother Nature, while providing roughly 56 cubic inches of interior space, yet only weighing 1.7 pounds. The Gobox 1 is ideal for kayakers, rafting enthusiasts, off-roaders, mountain bikers, and of course, hunters. And it’s available in five different colors.

There are about 101 different ways to repel mosquitoes but few are as effective and easy to use as a ThermaCELL. The lightweight MR300 uses a cylindrical fuel canister to gently warm a small metal plate, which in turn activates the material infused into the unit’s repellent mat. This repellent diffuses into a 15 square-foot zone of protection around the unit, keeping mosquitoes at bay within 15 minutes and (under optimal conditions) for as long as four hours. No batteries. No DEET sprays. And it’s safe to use around pets.

Firearm maintenance should be a concern of all hunters. Fortunately, the Allen Company’s Breakthrough Clean Technologies Universal Cleaning Kit helps make the job much easier. Included in the tough ammo can container are a variety of nylon brushes, brass patch jags, two multi-section cleaning rods, patches, swabs, a silicone gun cloth, solvent, and a bottle of ‘Battle Born’ high purity gun oil. It’s everything you need to clean a gun in one place.

Even in these modern times of precision air rifles, there’s still something to be said about Old School stuff — like Crosman’s 760 Pumpmaster air rifle. Now here’s a tool developed specifically to introduce young or new shooters to the shooting sports. The 760 handles both .177 caliber pellets and BBs, sending them downrange at 615/700 and 645 feet-per-second, respectively. A crossbolt safety, synthetic stock/fore-end, grooved receiver to accommodate a 4x scope, and traditional front/rear blade sights make this the perfect gift for all ages.

MercadoLibre Plunged After Earnings. Here's Why I'm Not Selling.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Latin American e-commerce and fintech leader grew revenue 45%, but higher spending squeezed its margins.

Shares of MercadoLibre (MELI -12.05%) fell by 12% on Nov. 5, the day after the company's third-quarter earnings came in below analysts' expectations. That leaves the stock more than 40% below its all-time high. Of course, some of it was due to broader market pressure, as the Nasdaq Composite had its worst day in years, but there were also numbers in the company's results that drove the decline. MercadoLibre is one of the largest investments in my own portfolio, so naturally, I'm not thrilled about the drop. But I'm not planning to sell a single share.

MercadoLibre's revenue grew 45% year over year to $10.75 billion, or 41% on a currency-neutral basis, slightly ahead of analysts' estimates. Its commerce business grew revenue 47%, and its fintech business grew 42%.

The underlying metrics were strong, too. Gross merchandise volume (GMV), the total value of goods sold on its marketplace, rose 33% on a currency-neutral basis. Total payment volume through Mercado Pago grew 53% to $109 billion, and the fintech platform now has about 93 million monthly active users, up 29% from a year ago.

In a nutshell, the problem was profitability. MercadoLibre earned $8.83 per share in the third quarter, below the roughly $9.30 analysts expected, and its operating margin shrank to 6.3% from 9.8% a year earlier.

Based on that, you might be wondering whether something is going wrong with the business, but I don't see any massive red flags. MercadoLibre increased its fulfillment capacity by almost 20% from the second quarter, offered free shipping on more orders, and increased marketing spending in Brazil. As CFO Martín de los Santos explained on the call, "The quarter when you are building capacity, you don't have the revenues yet, so you have the cost." He also said he expects margins to recover next quarter.

But the two biggest reasons MercadoLibre's operating margin fell year-over-year were higher operating costs and bad debts from the rapidly growing credit portfolio.

Speaking of the credit portfolio, it's also worth noting that side of the business held up. MercadoLibre's loan portfolio grew 62% to $17.9 billion, and the rate of loans 15 to 90 days past due was 7%, roughly unchanged from the prior quarter and close to historical lows, according to management. Management also said it's being a bit more conservative with new loans in Brazil and Argentina, which I like to see when a loan book is growing this quickly. There were higher rates of bad debt, but that's a function of the rapid growth of the credit portfolio, not of deteriorating credit quality.

I own MercadoLibre because it's the dominant e-commerce and fintech platform in Latin America, it's still growing far faster than most large companies, and its logistics network would be very difficult for a competitor to replicate. Nothing in this report changes any of that. In fact, the biggest reason profits came in light is that the company is widening that logistics advantage. After the drop, the stock trades for less than 40 times forward earnings, well below its five-year average of about 63 times. Source

To be sure, there are risks. If free shipping and heavier marketing turn out to be permanent costs rather than investments, margins could remain lower for longer. And a lending portfolio growing 62% a year would be the first place to show stress if Latin American consumers hit a rough patch. I'll be watching both closely, especially whether margins recover next quarter as management expects.

Having said all that, a company growing revenue 45% doesn't become less valuable because it chose to spend more in one quarter. If anything, I'm more likely to add to my position than to sell.

Matt Frankel, CFP® has positions in MercadoLibre. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends MercadoLibre. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Attacked by Camino Cows

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

As we left Sarria at 7:30 AM, we immediately joined the throngs of new pilgrims embarking on a weekend hike. To earn a Compostela certificate, rules dictate that you must complete at least the final 100 kilometers to Santiago. Today’s trek cover 14 miles under overcast skies, with cool, comfortable temperatures hovering in the high 60s.

We began our morning by climbing a hill toward the oldest section of the city, where many albergues are concentrated. At the crest, the city ended abruptly. A quick descent brought us right back out into the open countryside.

From there, the trail climbed steeply along a beautifully wooded path. Lined with massive trees, it resembled an ancient road cut through a dense forest. A steady stream of pilgrims climbed alongside us in the misty morning air. Looking ahead across the open fields, walkers stretched as far as the eye could see. After Sarria, the Camino feels noticeably different–much more like a bustling tourist event.

In the small Hamlet of Vilei, I found Jesus! (the small plastic Jesus often left along trails). I tucked him into my pocket, and just like that, Jesus joined us for the journey.

The trail continued uphill towards the towns of Barbadelo and Serra, winding along dirt paths that cut through cow pastures. Along the way, we passed ancient trees with trunks spanning up to 15 feet in diameter. The lingering mist added a mysterious quality to the woods. For miles at a time, stone walls framed our route past quiet orchards and tree farms.

As we navigated the mostly flat terrain between small villages, short climbs and descents kept us moving forward. In several towns, local herders guided cattle through narrow medieval streets. These cows had impressive horns and felt intimidating as they brushed past just inches away. On multiple occasions, we had to use our trekking poles to gently nudge them away from our bodies.

Eventually, we crossed a peaceful creek via an old stone walking bridge. The sudden influx of weekend pilgrims was lively and loud, creating a sharp contrast to the quiet solitude of our earlier weeks. To manage the distraction, we turned inward and focused on our rhythm. When we checked our progress, we were shocked to realize we had covered 7 miles in just two hours!

Right past the creek stood a tree covered in memorials dedicated to loved ones. I stopped to scan the cards, viewing names, dates and faces–including a photo of a young man at just 20 years old. Taking a moment to pray and reflect, I felt immense gratitude not only for this journey, but for all the years that God has given me.

Throughout this stretch of the Camino, I noticed tall, narrow stone structures perched up on elevated stilts. At first, they looked like elaborate decorations, roughly the size of a coffin. After seeing dozens of them across various farming villages, my curiosity got the better of me. I snapped a photo and asked AI to identify them.

It turns out these structures are traditional granaries (horreos) us to store grain. They are raised five feet off the ground to keep rodents out, featured slated stone walls to allow airflow while keeping the crop dry. Many were surprisingly ornate, and we continued seeing them for weeks to come.

We stopped for coffee in the small hamlet of Morgade, where we ran into several pilgrims we haven’t seen in days. Many looked weary from the sudden crowd of weekend hikers. Soon after, our “tramily”, Robert and Jill, popped into the same café.

We ran into some familiar faces today! First, we bumped into Simon—our long-lost friend from the very beginning of this hike.

Better yet, we also crossed paths with Manuel, our new friend from Portugal. Manuel is a seasoned pilgrim who has hiked numerous Caminos. What makes Manuel unforgettable is the heavy collection of Camino trinkets he carries from every single journey he has completed.

Reunited, with our tramily, we set off together for the final push into Portomarin.

Emerging into an open clearing on a high ridge, we were surprised by the mountainous terrain still visible in the distance. Below us in the valley stretch the broad river, with now forms a massive reservoir lake.

To reach the water, we navigated a steep, narrow path eroded into the hillside and boxed in by 10-foot stone walls. The descent required careful footing across loose, large rocks. At the bottom, the trail met a paved road leading to a long, modern bridge spanning the river into Portomarin.

Portomarin was completely relocated to the hillside in the 1950s when the river was dammed o create the reservoir. As a result, the town lost much of its historic feel, even though several historic structures—including the main cathedral—were moved brick by brick.

Unlike medieval villages with narrow, winding alleys, new Portomarin features wide, straight streets line with uniform concrete paving. The layout gives the town a modern, resort-like feel, amplified by the crowds finishing their 100 kilometer leg.

We gathered with our tramily for food and drinks before retiring to our plain, modern albergue for the night. Reflecting on the day, we missed the quiet intimacy of the trail prior to Sarria, and we agreed that we wouldn’t repeat this final 100 kilometer section on future trips.

The buyer pays the same price as they would otherwise, and your purchase helps to support The Trek's ongoing goal to serve you quality backpacking advice and information. Thanks for your support!

After decades of no backpacking an abrupt life incident forced me back into the woods for some shinrin-yoku where I ran into a NOBO tramily at Shuckstack Fire Tower. That started an idea of a longer term forest bath on the AT. What a long strange trip its been. Any financial donations I get will be used to send out love to other thru hikers a trail magic.

Liverpool and Chelsea are reportedly among the biggest suitors for the transfer of RB Leipzig centre-back Castello Lukeba.

Other top clubs such as Manchester United and Barcelona have also been mentioned as teams keeping a close eye on Lukeba’s situation at Leipzig, as per journalist Ekrem Konur.

We’ve previously seen Lukeba linked with big moves, with Man Utd also mentioned as having pursued him in the summer, when Football Transfers reported that he had an €80m release clause.

Now there’s been another update as Ekrem Konur has posted on X about clubs circling for Lukeba in what could end up being a major transfer battle for the Frenchman…

“RB Leipzig centre-back Castello Lukeba has emerged as one of the top defensive targets for several European giants,” the Turkish journalist posted. “Liverpool, Chelsea, Manchester United, Bayern Munich and Barcelona are all reportedly considering the French defender as a priority option for their backlines.

“Lukeba’s athleticism, ball-playing ability and long-term potential continue to attract strong interest from across Europe. A major battle for Castello Lukeba’s signature could develop in the upcoming transfer windows.”

It will be interesting to see how this pans out, as Lukeba looks like he could certainly be a strong signing for the likes of Chelsea and Liverpool.

Both of those clubs could do with bringing in a new centre-back, and we’ve been made aware of AC Milan’s Koni De Winter also being of interest, as reported exclusively via the Daily Briefing earlier today.

Lukeba is another option who’d surely help LFC as they look for a long-term replacement for the ageing Virgil van Dijk, while CFC look in need of upgrades on inconsistent performers like Wesley Fofana in their backline, while summer signing Maxence Lacroix has also had a mixed start and is now injured.

It seems inevitable that Lukeba will leave Leipzig at some point, with the Bundesliga side often tending to cash in on their star names at some point, and usually for a significant profit.

The 23-year-old should now be approaching his peak years, and he’ll surely be keen to represent a bigger club and perhaps test himself in the Premier League.

Everything to know about Best Buys Black Friday sale: Early deals, price matching, store hours, and more

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It's time to start hatching a plan for Black Friday 2026. Most major retailers treat the entire month like one drawn-out holiday sale, and Best Buy is no exception. The electronics retailer has announced plans to roll out two waves of early Black Friday deals before a 10-day savings extravaganza around Black Friday proper (Nov. 27).

Having covered Black Friday sales for nearly a decade, Mashable's reporters and editors agree that Best Buy is the place to shop if you're looking for Black Friday deals on big-ticket items like large TVs, Windows laptops, gaming consoles, and espresso machines. This year, the pressure is on for it to combat price hikes related to the global memory crisis, which has made many popular tech gifts way more expensive than usual.

Here's everything you need to know about Best Buy's Black Friday sale before you start shopping.

Best Buy's Black Friday sale will run from Thursday, Nov. 19 through Saturday, Nov. 28. Paid members of the My Best Buy program (Plus and Total tiers) will earn double points on their purchases from Nov. 19 to Dec. 6, the Sunday after Cyber Monday. Those points are redeemable for reward certificates.

Some of Best Buy's Black Friday deals will be available ahead of time. The retailer is releasing batches of Early Black Friday Deals and Doorbusters on Friday, Nov. 6 and Nov. 13 as a way of "giving shoppers an early jump on some of the season’s best offers," per a Best Buy blog post.

It's smart to shop Best Buy's Black Friday deals as early as possible: According to a company FAQ page, items marked with its "Black Friday Deal" badge are available in limited quantities and do not offer rainchecks. "When they are sold out, they may be sold out through Black Friday," it notes.

We expect high-demand, highly giftable items like Pokémon cards and gaming consoles (especially special-edition variants) to sell out the fastest.

Best Buy hasn't announced its store hours for Black Friday 2026 yet, but last year it opened at 6 a.m. local time.

No, Best Buy stores will be closed on Thanksgiving Day, which falls on Thursday, Nov. 26 this year. This has been its policy since 2020. (Same with Target, Walmart, and other major retailers.) The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

It might be worth trekking to your local Best Buy on Black Friday. According to some fine print on the company's Black Friday landing page, "there are usually a small handful of Black Friday deals that are only available for shoppers in a physical Best Buy store." We don't know exactly what those are yet, but we'll update Mashable readers as soon as we find out.

If you'd rather avoid the stress of navigating crowds and lines, you won't miss out on much by staying home. Most of Best Buy's Black Friday deals will be available on its website, it said.

Best Buy normally price-matches competing retailers such as Amazon, Costco, The Home Depot, Target, and Walmart. However, it changes this policy during the holidays. It will not price-match any official Black Friday deals at other retailers, nor any items on sale elsewhere from the Thursday before Thanksgiving through Cyber Monday (whether they're called Black Friday deals or not).

So, if another retailer offers a better Black Friday deal on an item you've already purchased from Best Buy, you'll just have to return it and buy it again.

The only deals Best Buy will price-match on Black Friday are its own. If you buy something there sometime between now and Dec. 31, and it gets cheaper later on in the season, Best Buy will refund the difference. You can request price matches online and in stores.

Some items are excluded from Best Buy's Holiday Price Match Guarantee, including holiday decorations, "activatable" devices purchased under a third-party contract (such as phones), and non-returnable products like trading cards, gift cards, and e-bikes. Items sold by third-party sellers, aka "Marketplace Sellers," are also ineligible for price matches.

Almost anything you purchase at Best Buy now through Dec. 31 can be returned or exchanged until Jan. 15, 2027. (If you're a My Best Buy Plus or Total member, you've got until Jan. 31, 2027.)

Again, the main exceptions here are holiday decor and activatable devices. They have 14- and 15-day return windows, respectively.

Best Buy is having a Cyber Sunday and Monday Sale Event on Nov. 29 and 30. It will then offer Cyber Week Deals from Dec. 1 to 6.

To wrap up the holidays, Best Buy will have a Last-Minute Savings Event from Dec. 18 to 24.

Haley Henschel is a Chicago-based Senior Shopping Reporter at Mashable who reviews and finds deals on popular tech, from laptops to gaming consoles and VPNs. She has years of experience covering shopping holidays and can tell you what’s actually worth buying on Black Friday and Amazon Prime Day. Her work has also explored the driving forces behind digital trends within the shopping sphere, from dupes to 12-foot skeletons.

First Fat Bear Week winner remembered in short film

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 6, 2026 1:19 PM EST

Once upon a time, there lived a fat bear. Bear #480, lovingly dubbed Otis, captured millions of hearts, as the first winner of Fat Bear Week over 10 years ago. For weeks, people tuned in to watch a popular livestream of him and the other bears at Katmai National Park and Preserve in Alaska.

Today, explore.org released a video tribute in honor of this brown bear (Ursus arctos) ambassador. Love Letters to Otis begins in 2013 with one of his first appearances on the livestream, and chronicles how Otis provided entertainment for those at home during the COVID-19 pandemic lockdowns.

The short documentary even features some of the Otis-inspired artwork fans sent to the Katmai rangers, and shows off this zen bear’s distinct fishing style, along with some of the physical challenges he faced in his later years. Otis died in 2024. While we don’t know the exact cause of death, since these bears do not wear tracking collars, rangers suspect old age or natural causes.

“His steadfast patience and determination when fishing at Brooks Falls gave many people a sense of calm,” Mike Fitz, a former National Park Service (NPS) ranger and explore.org naturalist told Popular Science in September. “He was an ambassador for his species who helped people fall in love with Brooks River and its bears and, in turn, [worked] to protect the salmon runs that sustain the Bristol Bay ecosystem.”

Explore.org Director of New Media Candice Rusch added that she has a particularly strong memory of Otis fishing in Brooks Falls in October 2021.

“All the bears had mostly left the river and there was a massive snow storm,” she reflected. “Otis, calm as anything, sat in the river, in the snow, catching fish.”

In 2014, Otis was crowned the first winner of Fat Bear Tuesday, which would go on to become the popular Fat Bear Week. He wore the Fat Bear Week crown three more times (2016, 2017, and 2021).

He was often seen at favorite spot in Brooks Falls, known as “Otis’s Office,” a pool on the river on the left side of the falls.

The brown bears return to Brooks Falls for the salmon migration every year in late June. There, they fatten up for the long winter ahead. By late summer, the nutritious fish spawn and begin to die. The bears then move to the lower Brooks River in September and October to eat the dead and dying salmon near the river’s mouth.

Even two years after Otis’ death, he remains a beloved symbol of the park and its chunky bears. Brown bears like him can live up to 34 years in the wild, but males generally live 22 years.

His legacy lives on through the Otis Fund, an annual fall fundraiser that raises money to support Katmai’s research, conservation, and educational outreach programs. Since 2020, the Otis Fund has raised over $4 million.
